Where French Rioters are Born

French history through the lens of a little town

Where French Rioters are Born

French history through the lens of a little town

David Roman

Nov 8

READ IN APP

France has been in the news because of all the wrong reasons, to the point that it’s hard for younger people to comprehend just how appealing and magnetic the country and its culture were just a few decades ago. And how different.

Take the latest round of riots over this fall, amply supported by La France Insoumise, the open borders bloc mostly in control of the French Left and one more-or-less backed by the now nearly defunct French Communist Party, or PCF in French. The comparison looks unavoidable with those of May 1968, save for one small detail: the PCF had a major role not in fueling, but in stopping the riots of May 1968; and not only that: the PCF was very much against mass immigration for most of its history, arguing that big business was very much in favor because of concerns about rising worker wages and lowered corporate margins.

A History of Mankind is a reader-supported publication. To receive new posts and support my work, consider becoming a free or paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

To understand how and why that changed we can have a look at a small town called Vitry-sur-Seine, nor far from Paris, a leftist stronghold that became notorious across the country when its Communist mayor bulldozed the homes of immigrants during Christmas, 1980. This was called the “Bulldozer Affair.”

(For those intrigued about that now-entirely-forgotten Communist mayor who bulldozed immigrant homes, here’s a contemporary video report about the event, en Français).

The scandal caused by the, let’s say, proactive attitude of the Commie mayor was mostly among the right. The very next year, PCF secretary general Georges Marchais run for the presidency on the slogan “Stop immigration, official and illegal.” This was the same Georges Marchais who, on May 3, 1968, the very day the May revolt began, dismissed its leader Daniel Cohn-Bendit in the pages of L’Humanité, the PCF paper, as a “German anarchist”: which Cohn-Bendit, to be perfectly honest, was.

As I write these lines, the idea that the working class has been deserting leftist parties across Western nations, as supposedly progressive movements turn into lobbying groups for Big Capital and sexual and religious minorities, is widespread. The far-right party of Marine Le Pen is hoping to reach the presidency next year on the strength of such voters, precisely. But this is an old story.

Consider for example that the pivotal December 2019 election in the U.K., the one that made Brexit a no-way-out proposition, was decided by the same voting trend. This was the first time in which the Conservatives grabbed an equal share of the British lower-class vote in any election ever.

Matt Goodwin

@GoodwinMJ

David Roman

Sep 23

Read full story

So, what’s going on these days in Vitry-sur-Seine, I hear you ask?

A mere ten kilometers south of central Paris, this is a municipality with 93,000 people as of the latest census. It’s not pretty: I was over there at the turn of the millennium, and it reminded me a lot of the rough-and-tumble districts west of downtown Madrid where I spent much of my childhood, escaping sneaker thieves and junkies chasing youngsters for pocket money.

A lot of things have changed since 1980 in French politics. One of the most remarkable is the conversion of the PCF, once a working-class redoubt, into a globalist, pro-open borders, decidedly non-Marxist party. Whenever you come across a piece of news such as this about a long-time helper of illegal immigrants who was murdered by one, you know the dude (or, very often, lady) is a Leftist through and through.

One would think this is the kiss of death for a party in a town crammed full of housing projects where nobody ever goes on shopping holidays, right? You may be surprised to learn that it hasn’t. The PCF has simply elected a new constituency.

France’s 1999 census found that about 30% of Vitry-sur-Seine residents were born outside of France (this is not counting, of course, descendants of immigrants from the 1960s and 1970s whose houses were bulldozed by the Communist mayor) and the number probably is now closer to 40%. Unemployment is around 25%, over twice the French average. There are several mosques.

The bulldozing scandal now forgotten, Vitry-sur-Seine is locally famous as the picturesque place where Muslim teenager Sohane Benziane was burned alive in 2002 by her jealous Muslim boyfriend, an act that led to the creation of the international feminist movement “Ni putes ni soumises” (remember the name of the French leftist bloc, La France Insoumise.) Not many old-stock French, of the kind who voted PCF in 1970, are left in Vitry-sur-Seine.

It’s a new world. So, here’s the list of all the mayors in Vitry-sur-Seine since Liberation from Nazi rule:

The PCF has found a way to stay in power. It only had to betray Marxism, but that’s no big sacrifice. Old Commies complain, but they will be dead in no time, while Vitry’s youngsters have had a few days to blow off some steam and enjoy themselves breaking and burning stuff in Paris.

It’s almost back to normal already and the PCF will keep working hard to guarantee a steady supply of cheap maids and gardeners for the upper classes, commuting to and from the big city every day. And, if they riot every once in a while, well: that’s the cost of doing business, and that’s what insurance is for.

What Happens Inside a Looped Transformer?

How reusing Transformer layers changes computation, reasoning, and efficiency, from early algorithms to adaptive depth. ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

November 07, 2026 | Read online

∇ Guide: Looped Transformers: when is another pass worth the cost?

How reusing Transformer layers changes computation, reasoning, and efficiency, from early algorithms to adaptive depth.

Ksenia Se & Alyona Vert.

Recently, there was a research boom on Looped Transformers – and they keep multiplying. Why now? We are at a point where we need to rethink familiar AI architectures to uncover their full potential, and in the case of Looped Transformers – the potential of reusing layers. As the whole AI stack matures, its basic foundations need to meet the demands of new systems.

In a conventional Transformer, depth is easy to picture. An input passes through a sequence of layers, each usually with its own weights, before reaching an output. Making that stack deeper gives the model more transformations to perform, but also more parameters to store and train. The amount of computation and the size of the model grow together.

Looped Transformers loosen this connection. They send a hidden representation through the same layers, updating it on each pass, and do it again and again in a loop. The benefit here is that a model can therefore do more work without acquiring a new set of weights for every additional step. That sounds perfect, but everything is not that simple.

So this guide will be useful for you to deeply understand what Looped Transformers are, what happens inside the loop, how to optimize and track the efficiency of your looped model in 2026. And most importantly: when is another pass worth the cost?

In today’s episode:

Essential definitions

What travels around the Transformer Loop

The architecture of Looped Transformers: distinct blocks versus repeated applications

Changing the state on each pass

Why Looped Transformers became important for algorithms

Adaptive Depth: deciding where to spend another pass

Does looping actually save resources?

What happens to hardware, KV cache and other parameters?

Conclusion

Sources and further reading

Essential definitions

First of all, let’s start with the widely used definitions that will help you feel fluent in the topic of Looped Transformers:

Looped Transformer: A Transformer that runs the same block or stack of blocks multiple times, updating its hidden state on each pass.

Transformer block: A unit containing attention and a feed-forward network, usually with residual connections and normalization.

Hidden state: The numerical representation of the tokens as the model processes them. These representations, not newly generated words, travel through the loop.

Weight sharing: Reusing the same parameters across multiple passes instead of storing a separate set for each one.

Loop / recurrence: One pass through the shared block or stack. The output becomes the input for another pass.

Recurrent depth: In this context, the number of times the shared block or stack runs. Terminology varies across papers: some use “depth” to count individual block applications instead.

Input injection: Adding or otherwise reintroducing the original input representation during repeated passes, so the model can keep using it as its hidden state changes.

Adaptive depth: Giving different inputs or tokens different numbers of layers or passes, rather than using the same fixed number for everything.

Effective depth: The total number of block applications. For example, four blocks repeated eight times give 32 applications; any blocks outside the loop are counted separately.

Full-stack looping: Repeating the entire Transformer stack.

Middle-stack looping: Repeating a core group of blocks between input and output blocks that run once.

What travels around the Transformer Loop

A Looped Transformer reuses one Transformer block, or a stack of blocks, across several passes. Each block contains familiar components: attention, a feed-forward network, and residual connections. The distinct architectural change is that the output of the shared stack becomes its input on the next pass.

Image Credit: Universal Transformers

The Architecture of Looped Transformers: Distinct blocks vs. Repeated applications

Standard vs. looped Transformers at the same effective depth

In this example

Standard Transformer

Looped Transformer

Distinct blocks with their own weights

Passes through the stack

Total block applications

Where does the hidden state go after block six on the first pass?

Forward to block seven

Back to block one for another pass

Our example produces 24 block applications, using six sets of unique block weights. This makes visible two separated quantities:

Unique depth counts the distinct blocks whose weights the model stores.

Effective depth counts how many block applications the input passes through.

For a fully repeated stack, effective depth equals the number of unique blocks multiplied by the number of passes. If the model has an unrepeated beginning and end, those blocks are counted once.

Note that it doesn't become equivalent to a conventional 24-block model, because the latter has more freedom to specialize each depth. Looping's aim is to win additional computation without an additional set of block weights for every pass. Let’s explore all of it in depth.

Changing the State on Each Pass

When is another pass worth it? In the full guide, we follow the hidden state through the loop, compare ways to decide when to stop, and see why a smaller KV cache can let a GPU serve more requests at once. We also cover the most important 2026 breakthroughs.

Model Convo: Ismail Kurun

On World Models, World Music, and Embodied Judgment

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Model Convo: Ismail Kurun

On World Models, World Music, and Embodied Judgment

Ryan Hauser

Nov 6

READ IN APP

Ismail Kurun

This week’s convo features Ismail Kurun, a fellow at VALIANT, Vanderbilt University’s interdisciplinary AI lab. Kurun works at the nexus of AI and PPE (philosophy, politics, and economics) and is a Paul Lewis Fellow at the Mercatus Center. You can follow his work on X and PhilPeople.

How did you get into emerging tech? What are you working on now?

I have followed emerging tech for a long time. I was blogging about cryptocurrencies back in 2014 because I was interested in blockchain’s decentralized architecture. Then I got interested in AI through my work in political philosophy and mainline political economy. My research investigates how societies sustain or develop formal and informal liberal institutions, particularly epistemically. The transition to the AI economy is putting pressure on these institutions. In 2024, I joined VALIANT to work on this.

I am currently most concerned with markets and the practices of public justification that support democratic decision-making. My recent paper on AI and markets contributes to the economic calculation debate. I ask whether AI systems can reproduce the epistemic functions of markets. And I argue that entrepreneurial discovery in particular resists computational replication. It depends on embodied, historically situated judgment rather than computation alone.

What work of art has most shaped your views on emerging tech?

Jules Verne’s books. I read them when I was a kid. They tied technology and adventure together in my mind at a young age and made me curious about the future in a way I never really outgrew.

Scenes from the novels of Jules Verne. | Neuville & Riou

What’s your most contrarian take on AI or emerging tech policy?

My most contrarian take on AI is that the loudest disagreement in AI is less fundamental than it looks.

Accelerationists and existential risk theorists disagree about whether the arrival of AI systems matching or exceeding human capability will go well, and about how fast we should move toward it. But they share the assumption that competence is separable from the kind of being that has it.

But researchers working on embodied AI and world models argue that grounding in sensorimotor experience is necessary for general competence, that you cannot achieve general competence through disembodied prediction alone, however much text you train on.

They treat the body as an engineering problem. If you build the sensors and run the robot, then it will be grounded. But that solves only half the problem. You can build sensors, but how do you build judgment?

Entrepreneurial judgment is a case in point. It is tacit, something you must live through. Judgment and even knowing what’s worth attending to both come about when people with skin in the game have decision-making power and when dissent is tolerated. Judgment withers when these conditions are missing.

A (failed) experiment in economic calculation. | Chile Primero

The rules that matter here are not only the formal ones but the informal —— commercial norms, trust, and the shared expectations that make formal rules work at all. These are learned through participation, which is why transplanted institutions often fail to take root.

So, questions about AI capability become questions of institutional survival. And if AI drives down the cost of execution while leaving judgment where it is, orders that are good at producing judgment (i.e., open societies) should see their advantage over others widen. But the same technology strains those orders, so the outcome depends on whether we can renew them faster than AI erodes them.

What worries me in the meantime is a version of what Hayek called the pretence of knowledge: the assumption that because AI is impressive, we already know which human functions it can replace.

To be clear, I’m optimistic and excited about AI’s potential. But my worry is that, because of that assumption, we hollow out the institutions that have produced so much human progress, including AI, over the past few centuries.

What are you reading right now?

First, there is the technical literature on world models, Yann LeCun’s joint embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) line in particular, and on the vision-language-action (VLA) policies coming out of robotics. This is one direction the economic calculation debate may go next.

Then there are the interpretive and cultural foundations of market processes. I want to understand how growing up in different commercial cultures might change how a person sees business opportunities. So, I am reading Virgil Storr’s Understanding the Culture of Markets and Don Lavoie’s essays on hermeneutics.

Both strands of reading bring me back to the same questions. What kinds of knowledge can be automated? What kinds are generated through participation in markets and other liberal institutions? And what happens to those institutions when AI is introduced to them?

Go-to emerging tech music track?

Well, I like ethnic, religious, or otherwise culturally significant music, much like Tyler Cowen. I have a large collection of world music —— from Balkan Roma and the muwashshahat of Arab Andalusia to Greek Orthodox hymns and Vedic chants.

There is a great deal to be gained from “cracking cultural codes,” from immersing yourself in unfamiliar forms of life to understand and appreciate the aspirations of different cultures.

I have talked about this in more detail in an interview for the American Philosophical Association’s blog. Liberalism has drawn on a humanistic tradition concerned with understanding cultures from within. This is a line running from Alexander von Humboldt through Isaiah Berlin. I share that spirit.

Edited for length and clarity, with hyperlinks added.

It’s Ok to Believe Again

Trump has lost the country. What comes next is up to us.

Jon Favreau

Nov 7

Preview

READ IN APP

Tuesday was the most hopeful I’ve felt about politics since 2008.

I had a feeling we’d win, and win big – a feeling I didn’t completely trust, given everything that’s happened over the last decade, but I couldn’t shake it. It wasn’t just the polls, which I obsessed over like the true sicko that I am. It wasn’t just the war and the tariffs and the ICE raids. It wasn’t just the corrupt buffoons in charge who constantly advertise how little they give a shit about anyone but themselves and their egos and their money.

What I felt was that something had finally changed in America. That people were finally done. The Trump Show got old years ago, but it became intolerable to watch once the president and his party were the reason you couldn’t afford food or gas or rent, or the reason your kid was sent to the Middle East, or the reason your neighbor disappeared one day. It was no longer just an embarrassment you could turn away from, but a nightmare you couldn’t escape.

It shouldn’t have taken this long. There has been too much pain and suffering and cruelty that too many people allowed. There will probably be more. Two years is a lifetime and the President of the United States is the most powerful person on the planet.

But Donald Trump has lost the country. ...

Dems Aren’t Backing Off Their Own Redistricting Push

Even after their big wins, party officials eye new maps—both to expand their House advantage and to warn Republicans to back off.

Joe Perticone, Sam Stein • FREE PREVIEW

READ TO ME

Good morning and welcome to a special edition of Press Pass. If you thought The Bulwark would take a night off after the elections, you’re sorely mistaken. Our work never stops because we love you, our readers. If you want to join us and our growing online community, consider upgrading to a Bulwark+ membership with a 20 percent discount thanks to our post-election flash sale:

Get 20% off for 1 year

Today’s special edition of the newsletter is about how Democrats, fresh from giant victories in the midterm elections, are going on offense. Partisan and intraparty battles over mid-decade redistricting have started up again: Many Democrats are ready to push for further congressional map consolidation in advance of the next election cycle, while others are holding out the possibility of new maps as a threat to ward off Republican states that are considering squeezing a few more seats out of their own electorates. Read all about it below.

(Photo illustration by Bill Kuchman/The Bulwark | Photos: Shutterstock)

TUESDAY’S MASSIVE MIDTERM VICTORIES catapulted Democrats to control of the House, the Senate, governors’ mansions, and state legislatures across the country. But the party isn’t content with the once-in-a-generation shift in its favor. In fact, officials are already pondering ways to win more.

Several Democratic officials have told The Bulwark that they remain committed to carrying on mid-decade redistricting efforts that the party started but put on ice over the past year. Calling it a top agenda item, they characterized it as both an offensive effort to win more House seats and a defensive measure in case Republicans continue to push for redistricting in GOP-controlled states.

5 tips for getting the most out of AI

5 tips for getting the most out of AI. Why do some people seem to be getting a lot out of AI , while others—maybe you—still feel like they’re spinning their wheels trying to squeeze anything remotely useful out of these platforms? The answer is multifaceted, of course. Some workflows are better suited to the strengths of large language models, while others are more vulnerable to their weaknesses. Some users pay for upgraded plans, which can improve how well the models work. But a big part of the difference comes down to how you use artificial intelligence. Like any tool, there are ways to make it perform better for you. In other words, the technology is often what you make of it. If you offload your thinking to AI platforms, you risk dulling your ability to approach problems creatively and becoming dependent on a flawed technological crutch. But if you use AI to challenge yourself, stress-test your work, and outsource routine tasks, you might feel, at least in some sense, like you’ve gotten a significant upgrade. Consider the calculator: Some people have become so reliant on it that their mental math skills suffer, while others use the technology to tackle more advanced math because they’re not spending their time on simpler problems. Like all new technologies, what you choose to outsource to AI is up to you. Still, if you do decide to use it, it’s worth considering how to get the most out of the technology—and make sure chatbots serve you , rather than the other way around. Here are a few tips to make these platforms work better for you. Be smart about your prompts What you type into your AI actually matters. Take traditional search engines. You may not realize it, but over time, you might have developed an implicit sense of how using certain Boolean operators or more specific proper nouns can yield better, more relevant results to your queries. The same is true for AI. Being more specific with a chatbot about what information you want can make a big difference, particularly when you’re using the technology as a search tool. It might be easy to slip into thinking that AI should already know what you want, but it doesn’t. Instead, treat a chatbot as an earnest research assistant with the capacity to make a lot of mistakes. There’s an old adage: “The more you know, the more they know.” The same logic applies to AI. Here’s an example: If I’m shopping for a bookshelf, I no longer sift through countless listings searching for something that feels right. Instead, I take a picture of the spot where I need it to go, give the AI the dimensions (or range of dimensions) I’m looking for, and ask the chatbot to find the best options given those constraints. This saves me a lot of time and allows me to work smarter when using AI for shopping. Encourage humility One of the big problems with AI—or any work you don’t do yourself and instead outsource to someone (or some thing ) else—is that it will make mistakes. Fear of an AI oopsie is anxiety-inducing a.

AlpineBSD Announced - an AI-free FOSS OS

Alpine BSD Announced - an AI-free FOSS OS. Lproven (Slashdot reader #6,030) shares his article about a new OS in the BSD family "combining Net BSD's kernel and libc with a port of the Alpine Linux userland" — and promising it won't use AI-generated code: Alpine BSD is so new that it barely exists yet. There are no installation media available, though it's possible to manually bootstrap an installation. What Alpine BSD does already have is an AI policy, and the plan is to avoid "the output of generative AI models (e.g. LLMs)" as far as possible: "Any software whose code, documentation, binaries, or other assets include, in any part, the output of a generative AI tool such as an LLM, is considered 'tainted' for our purposes. " Back in 2024, the Net BSD project made its position on AI plain. Its commit guidelines say that "code generated by a Large Language Model [. ] is presumed to be tainted code. " Alpine BSD's ports repository and source code are hosted on Sourcehut, under the account of sircmpwn, better known as Drew De Vault. We will follow Alpine BSD with interest. There's strong appeal in the plan of combining perhaps the best-known, longest-established AI-free Unix-like kernel with the user-facing parts of pretty much the smallest and cleanest Linux distro there is. The AI advocates often warn LLM skeptics that they risk being left behind. Well, here's an OS for those who don't want to surf the AI wave. The article does acknowledge difficulty installing software using the pkgin package manager (an optional extra), explaining that since 1993 the Net BSD community has had "its own ideas about what is normal. Some folks on the mailing list told us that they never use binary packages anyway — they compile what they need from source code." of this story at Slashdot. .

Microsoft Removes PC-to-PC Migration from Windows 11, Points Users to OneDrive-Based Backup

Microsoft Removes PC-to-PC Migration from Windows 11, Points Users to One Drive-Based Backup. Windows 11 offered a PC-to-PC Migration feature that "moved files and settings directly between two PCs over Wi-Fi or Ethernet," reports Ghacks. But according to an updated Microsoft support page, the tool is no longer available. "Microsoft's support documentation now redirects users to Windows Backup, a tool that relies on One Drive cloud storage instead of a direct connection between two computers. " Windows Backup works differently. It requires a personal Microsoft account and backs up supported folders (Desktop, Documents, Pictures, Videos and Music) to One Drive. Free One Drive accounts include only 5GB of storage, and Microsoft's own promotional screenshot for Windows Backup shows 4.5GB of that allowance already in use. Years of photos, documents and downloads can exceed the free tier quickly. Windows Backup does carry some extras. It can remember installed apps, Wi-Fi networks, passwords and some settings to speed up setup on a new device. Apps still have to be reinstalled, and supported files are restored from the cloud. Data Centers are Being Powered By Giant Gas Plants, Possibly Increasing Carbon Pollution for Decades

Data Centers are Being Powered By Giant Gas Plants, Possibly Increasing Carbon Pollution for Decades. Rather than racing toward their net-zero targets, Amazon and other AI/cloud computing companies "are driving emissions sharply higher," reports the Washington Post: To support their data centers, tech firms are driving construction of a new generation of supersized, gas-fired power plants that could lock in hundreds of millions of tons of climate pollution for decades to come, according to corporate disclosures and an analysis conducted for The Post by a researcher at the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health. Industry estimates show these projects have put the tech sector on course to grow from a modest driver of industrial carbon pollution into one of the world's biggest emitters of greenhouse gases. Some anticipate that its share of global carbon emissions will increase as much as fivefold from 2020 to 2030. That would leave the tech industry churning out more than half the climate pollution created by global aviation or shipping — sectors long associated with driving warming. Already, Amazon's carbon footprint reached 80.85 million metric tons last year — 58% larger than when [Amazon CEO Jeff] Bezos pledged to shrink it. The company has veered far from the path laid out in the Paris agreement, which would have emissions falling steeply by now and dropping to 43% below 2019 levels by 2030. The tech companies say in corporate disclosures and public statements that they remain committed to their net zero targets, and they suggest the emissions surge is temporary — to be mitigated down the road, when new energy technologies enable the data centers to run cleaner and more efficiently. They also stress that they continue to invest in clean energy and rank as some of the world's biggest consumers of it. With some complicated accounting maneuvers and carbon credit purchases, their solar, wind and geothermal installations partly cancel out their gas emissions, according to corporate disclosures. A June report from international insurance/asset management Allianz found the world's data center emissions are on track to double to 643 million tons of CO2 annually between 2020 and 2030, the article points out. And they cite American data centers contributing to the problem: Just two gas projects Amazon signed on to for data centers planned in Texas and Pennsylvania "would produce more carbon pollution than the dirtiest facility in the country," an Alabama coal plant powering 1 million homes. Meta is building a nearly six-square-mile campus in Louisiana "that will consume as much electricity as three cities the size of New Orleans." "A sprawling Microsoft project in West Texas involving a giant gas plant built by Chevron and a Google project in Wyoming that would consume five times as much electricity as every household in that state would vastly expand the emissions associated with those companies." RSS · 07 Nov 2026, 21:53

Is Mandating AI Use in the Workplace Backfiring?

Is Mandating AI Use in the Workplace Backfiring?. Some workplaces are holding mandatory AI training sessions, while others "are requiring employees to use a specific number of tokens," reports the Washington Post, "or log a certain amount of time on AI platforms." The article's headline? "Why mandating AI use in the workplace can backfire." [A] lot of employers are going about it the wrong way, said Arvind Karunakaran, an assistant professor of management science and engineering at Stanford University. "You can consume a lot of tokens without creating a lot of value," Karunakaran said. The adoption of AI is happening faster than the adoption of the internet did, according to a new report from Stanford's Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence. And many users report that AI tools have really helped — whether saving time and costs or increasing innovation. The same report, though, found that one-third of organizations surveyed expected AI-related staff reductions in the coming year. Especially in that context, simply mandating usage won't spark excitement about the technology, Karunakaran said, and neither will insisting that employees use AI to cut turnaround times and ramp up productivity. "People start asking questions like 'What am I as an employee getting out of it?'" he said. The report he co-authored found that in the short-term, mandating AI tools may ramp up productivity. But in the long-to-medium term, employees tend to feel devalued and disengaged. From there, people find ways to mindlessly check the box. When employers present these tools and their use as an opportunity for employees to expand their skills and grow their careers, people are much likelier to use them, Karunakaran said. He suggests that rather than requiring employees use a specific number of tokens, companies should encourage workers to experiment and share their own use cases and success stories. Most workplaces are still shutting employees out of the conversation. More than half of employees said they have less of a say than they'd want on adopting new tech at work, according to a report from Gallup and Jobs for the Future, a work and education nonprofit. Any Slashdot readers want to share your own stories on how AI was introduced at your workplace? . of this story at Slashdot. .

China's Top Spy Agency Recruited Woman Who Targeted Jensen Huang

China's Top Spy Agency Recruited Woman Who Targeted Jensen Huang. An anonymous reader quotes a report from The New York Times: A Chinese businesswoman who had a close relationship with the chief executive of one of the most important artificial intelligence companies in the United States was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, Beijing's top intelligence agency, according to people briefed on U.S. surveillance. The businesswoman, Alice Huang, was recruited by the ministry to collect intelligence on Jensen Huang, the chief executive of Nvidia, as part of a drive to learn more about American A.I. chips and potentially influence the company to push for its most advanced chips to be made available to China, the people said. [. ] The New York Times reported last week that Mr. Huang was a target of a Chinese intelligence operation, and that U.S. intelligence agencies were conducting electronic surveillance of Ms. Huang, a Chinese citizen, for at least a year. The Times reported that Mr. Trump had been briefed last year about Mr. Huang's relationship with her, but chose not to do anything about it. Ms. Huang speaks English well and has relationships with executives at the highest levels of the A.I. chip industry, including Mr. Huang and the leaders of Gigabyte, one of the top assemblers of Nvidia supercomputers, said people who have met her. She has attended industry conferences and private executive dinners. It is unclear when Ms. Huang first met Nvidia's leader. In 2024, around the time the two of them were photographed together at a bar in Taipei, her company, Megaspeed, began a multibillion-dollar purchase of advanced A.I. microchips. To many in the industry, the well-funded push by Megaspeed into A.I. appeared to come out of nowhere. Ms. Huang was connected to a network of companies and investors clustered around Shanghai and Zhejiang province that had used their ties to local officials in Shanghai to push aggressively from mobile video games into data centers, which provide the computing power behind artificial intelligence. Megaspeed has been building data centers in Southeast Asia and then renting their computing power to Alibaba, the giant Chinese internet firm, giving China a backdoor way of getting access to advanced Nvidia chips. Nvidia is restricted by the United States from selling those chips directly to China. Beyond its local connections, the network also has a financial link to the Chinese military establishment, through an investment in a cybersecurity subsidiary of China Electronics Technology Group, one of the country's largest makers of military technology. Following the report, top Republican lawmakers have asked Jensen Huang to appear before Congress to answer questions. "Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the People's Republic of China's access to sensitive US technology and information," representatives John Moolenaar, Brian Mast, and Rick Crawford said.
